# RIVENKEEP — THE LEGENDS: NOTES

## *The design companion to* The Book of the Riven Stone

*Notes v1.1.0 · for the Book v3.0.0 · 2026-10-02*

*This document sits beside the Book. The Book is the story and nothing else: the title page and Contents, Seren's foreword, the Invocation, the six Books with their Arguments, the tales with their datelines, the Epilogue and the Book of Knowings. Everything about how the Book was made, how its pieces fit, and how it enters the game is here. Nothing in this document is Book text, and nothing in it touches the simulation.*

*Notes v1.1.0 replaces Notes v1.0.0, which went with the Book v2.0.0. Its new first section records what v3 changed under Jack's notes of 2026-10-02. Every later section is brought up to the v3 Book: the cast, the deaths, the clock, the puzzle pieces, the delivery into the game, the names and the calls. The custom of burning, which v2.0 gave the coast, is gone from the Book and from these notes; it is named only where its removal is recorded (§1.8). The writers' and editors' notes of the v3 rewrite (`wf11/notes_inbox_v3.md`) are folded in, so this document can be read on its own.*

> **How to read the references.** "I.4" is a tale of the Book. "§5.3" is a section of this document. "v1.3" is Legends v1.3.0, "v2.0" is the Book v2.0.0, and "v3" is this Book, v3.0.0. "K4" is Jack's note number 4 of 2026-10-02 (the list is in §1.1). "J7" is his note number 7 of 2026-09-30 (§2.2). "Cam 12" is campaign 12. "B−75" is seventy-five years before the banner. A line in *italics* is quoted from the Book, word for word. "g01" to "g11" and "w01", "w02" are the thirteen hands that drafted v3 (§1.12).

## 0 · AT A GLANCE

1. **The Book is rewritten whole, in v1.3's voice, made hard by old English.** Jack rejected v2.0 as *"a bunch of run on sentences"*, *"clinical"*, *"like you are writing history text book"*, and asked for the earlier style, hard for a first-time reader, *"something that, if you read long enough, you would start to understand"* (K4). Research and a judged calibration settled how: Early Modern grammar in every line (*thou* and *ye*, *-eth*, *hath*, *spake*, *sware*, negation without *do*, the verb before the subject after a fronted word), a small stock of old words that keeps coming back, plain words at every hinge, and short clauses with full stops. The difficulty is in the grammar, never in the length: no sentence of the Book runs over 30 words (§1.2).
2. **At a master's length.** The Book runs 29,541 words leaf by leaf, against v2.0's 50,976 and v1.3's 26,095. The tales proper come to 27,945 words against ceilings of 29,350. Jack likes every element, so every element stays, and the words came out of the joints (K6; §1.3).
3. **The tales build.** Each raises one question by its hundredth word, shows what is at stake in action, tells one halt close and slow, turns on something the hearer has already seen, pays a cost in a line, and ends on a picture. Every death is planted and paid, and told whole in one place only (K9; §1.4).
4. **The frame is light.** A dateline of eight words or fewer says only when a tale was laid. A headnote of fifty words or fewer says who laid it, with one human touch. The *Hear how* openings, the records after the end and the glosses at the climax are gone. The Captain's hood goes back twice in the whole Book, and the strip of his cloak carries the other deaths.
5. **The wood leaves are pieced, then told.** Halyna give the memory up in fragments, half-thoughts and corrections, in two inks, as the wood gives it. Then Seren makes it a story in the wood's own *we*, in three rings (K5; §1.5).
6. **Seren is the master of the Book.** The tale is the teller's and the words are hers. Every teller hears his leaf read back and owns it, and the Book shows that lightly in three headnotes and in Idrenna's nod, and never says it (K5; §1.6).
7. **IV.3: Brenn only watched, and there is no custom of burning anywhere.** The warden, a weak man, had the boat burned to hide the deed. Every later tale that leaned on a custom now rests on plain fear of grey boats, spread by the survivors' tales, or drops the beat (K7, K8; §1.7, §1.8).
8. **The memory theme** is woven in lightly, in four nuances, with no new lore: remembering is chosen; a people is what it remembers; memory is handed down as rite; and the dead are remembered for their good as well as their loss (§1.9).
9. **Plain Words is a true retelling.** It was told again from each leaf's beats in easy modern English. It runs 84.8% of the Book's length, shares 13.6% of its four-word runs with the Book (v2.0's Plain shared 40.3%), reads at about grade 3, and keeps its own paragraphs (K4; §1.10).
10. **The tabs.** One switch changes the whole Book (K1). The Original must come back in every section, set down anew from v3 (K2). The copy Jack sends to others is the whole story on the web, with every stone and grain writing, and not a trimmed version (K3; §1.11).
11. **Untouched:** every song, Jack's fixed lines, the hearth's liturgy, the runners' oath, the warden's letter, the Guest's sentence and the Title. Every native key that v2.0 used is kept with its kind.
12. **The game's delivery keeps its laws and its schedule.** The changes are in §5: the wood leaf's reading now stands for "the shape" of a knowing; the hood is up except at IV.3 and the Epilogue; the hearth-words and Whispers quote the v3 wording; the interstitial excerpt goes back to 80 to 120 words; and the tabs keep the reader's place by shared anchors, since Plain Words now has its own paragraphs.
13. **Still open:** twenty-four calls for Jack, each reversible and each already applied as recommended (§7.2). The ones that most need an answer are Brenn's laughter, whether the Book is shared before its Original is set down, Hesk's strip, and *thee* in *"Hold it, and it will hold thee."*

## 1 · WHAT V3 CHANGED

Jack read v2.0 and its Plain Words on 2026-10-02 and turned both down. His nine notes are below, with what the Book does now. Behind them stand six research briefs (`wf11/research/R1`–`R6`), a calibration judged by four readers, two binding guides (`wf11/voice_v3.md` for the voice and `wf11/craft.md` for the build), the memory theme Jack approved the same day (`wf11/memory_theme.md`), and four model leaves (`wf11/samples/`). How the work was done is in §1.12.

### 1.1 Jack's notes of 2026-10-02, note by note

Each note is quoted from his own words in brief, then answered with what the Book does now and where. The orchestrator's reading notes, written for the writers the same day, are folded in where they settle a meaning.

**K1 · *"I liked how if you switched one tab in the story. The all changed."***
- Kept as a law. One switch changes the whole Book: every tale, the foreword, the Invocation, the Epilogue and the Book of Knowings, on the story page and in the game's reader alike. The choice is remembered with the settings (§1.11, §5.8).
- Because Plain Words now has its own paragraphs, switching keeps the reader's place by anchors both texts share, not paragraph by paragraph (§1.11).

**K2 · *"The original text is missing in all section. Bring that back."***
- v2.0's *Original* tab showed one quiet line, *Being set down anew in its own hands.*, because the Book had been rewritten and its Orrowen and grain had not. Jack reads that line as the text missing, and it is.
- The ruling: the *Original* tab must carry the leaf in its own hands in every section. The placeholder line is not a state to ship.
- The Original must be set down from v3, which is about 29,500 words of Book text against v2.0's 51,000, so the work is smaller than it was. It is the Tongues team's pass and is not part of this one. Until it lands, every native drawing the Book carries shows under *Original* at its place (§1.11, §5.8, §7.2 call 2).

**K3 · *"For sending this to someone else to read, I was looking for a way to habe them look on the web, not a trimmed down version of this document. This should be the full document with all the tree and stone writtings."***
- The copy Jack shares is the whole story page on the web: every leaf, all three tabs, and every native drawing in the course-hand, the Hal and the grain, with the facing leaves and the songs. It is private to the people he chooses. It is not a cut-down reader.
- These Notes are a separate document and are not part of the shared story unless Jack chooses (§1.11).

**K4 · *"The previous 'The Book' written style was much better. This new 'The Book' style is just a bunch of run on sentences. Bring back that style. I don't care is it is easier to read aloud. The Plain Word is just a change in punctuations with a little rephrasing. I wan t there to be a real difference, where the book would be hard to read to the uneducated or someone you hasn't read the text before. Something that, if you read long enough, you would start to understand. The Plain Word should be easy to read."***
- The Book has v1.3's sentence-craft and tellers' voices again, and it is made hard by Early Modern grammar and a small stock of old words that keep coming back, never by length (§1.2).
- The earlier register note (2026-09-30, *"I like the longer sentences joined together"*) is superseded for sentence length, and so is v2.0's choice to write for the voice.
- Plain Words was retold from the beats in easy modern English, and the measures show the real difference (§1.10).

**K5 · *"The ideas was that Seren loved the language and was able to be playful with it. Turning of phrase. painting pictures with it. While the stories are told by different people in different voices. She knows how to write them so they sound better. Ever story teller looks are her works and they all agree that her version is the best. For the wood speak, Halyna speaks in phrase and partial ideas as the wood tells the memory, They are piecing parts together. But Seren, she makes the memory a story."***
- Seren's play, her turns and her painted pictures are rules of the craft, and each tale has turns of her own (§1.6).
- Every teller's leaf is read back to him, and none has asked for his own words back. The Book shows that three times, lightly, and never says it (§1.6).
- Every wood leaf now opens with Halyna's pieced reading, in fragments and in two inks, and then Seren's story of the memory (§1.5).

**K6 · *"The stories are too long now. I like all of the elements in the story, but Seren would write with the economy of words, She is a master at her craft."***
- Every leaf has a ceiling, and every leaf is under it. The Book is 58% of v2.0's length (§1.3).
- No element Jack liked was cut to get there. The words came out of the joints between the beats, and what had to go is listed for his eye (§1.14).

**K7 · *"Eldhythe should not participate only observe. When he says 'we' he means as a whole group. He did not stop them. He was but a boy amoung the men."***
- "Eldhythe" here is Brenn, the teller of IV.3, whose confession was taken at the temple at Eldhythe.
- Brenn strikes no one and touches neither the Guest, nor the body, nor the boat, nor the fire. His *we* is the post. He says Jack's own sentence, in the Book's order: *I did not stop them. A boy was I among the men.* (§1.7).

**K8 · *"There is no customs of burn the boat. They did that to hide the evidence. They were cruel. This is an act of cruelty. This is the kind of stuff that muddies the story. make it less impactful, not better. This is a weak man."***
- The burning is the warden's own order, to hide the deed: *"Burn it," said he, "and shove it off, and give it the tide. Leave naught to show."* He is a weak man, shown only by his deeds (§1.7).
- There is no custom of burning anywhere in the Book. Each later tale that leaned on one rests now on plain fear of grey boats, which the survivors' tales spread, or the beat is gone (§1.8).
- The reading notes take *"make it less impactful, not better"* as the general rule: invented lore that explains a deed weakens it. The craft guide makes that rule 10 (*Light the deed and leave the heart dark. Never let a custom explain a deed.*).

**K9 · *"These stories just blah. they don't build. It all seem so clinical. How would JRR Tolkien tell this same story. How would any great writer tell a good story. You need to go an research this and follow what you learn. These are great stories that you made boring. It is like you are writing history text book. This is fiction and it is the funnest to write."***
- The research was done first (R1 Tolkien; R2 Homer, read with Auerbach; R3 economy and the archaic register; R4 how a story builds; R5 a measured diagnosis of v1.3 and v2.0; R6a and R6b, an inventory of every element of every tale).
- What it found is the craft guide, and every tale is built to it: a question, the stakes, one scene told close, a turn, a cost, a closing picture (§1.4).
- The chronicle habits R5 measured are gone: capitalised datelines, the *Hear how* openings, records after the end, name tags, glosses at the climax and endings given away in the headnote.

**And the memory theme.** Asked whether *We remember* could carry more, Jack answered the same day: *"Yes. Include them. It seems like we already have the elements and this just adds nuance to the story. That is the exact thing I was looking for."* The four nuances are woven into what the Book already held, with no new custom and no lecture (§1.9).

### 1.2 The register: the v1.3 voice in heavy old English

**What was wrong, measured.** R5 measured v1.3 and v2.0 the same way, on the tale bodies alone.
- v2.0 was run-on because of how its sentences were joined, not because they were old. Its mean sentence was 21 words against v1.3's 15. One sentence in eight ran past 45 words, against one in a hundred. It had 2.7 times v1.3's semicolons and twenty Homeric *As when…: so* similes of 60 to 76 words each.
- Its archaism was costume: *upon* (261 times) and *ere* (72) were nine-tenths of its old words, on modern grammar. So the difficulty lived in the length, and the Plain could only undo it by splitting sentences, which is exactly what Jack called *"a change in punctuations"*.
- v1.3 was not archaic either (0.9 old words in a thousand). What Jack liked in it was its sentence-craft: short and long mixed, few semicolons, each great moment on its own paragraph, and the sentences shortest at the crisis. Its tellers sounded like different men, and it never contracted.

**The research.** R3 found the way to be old without being long. The King James Bible has sounded old for four centuries, yet fewer than one word in a hundred in it is archaic: its age is in the grammar of every line (*thou*, *-eth*, *spake*, *knew not*). A grammar is a closed system that repeats in every sentence, so a reader learns it. Readers keep a strange word after about eight meetings and own it after twenty or thirty, so a small stock of old words, often met, teaches itself. Tolkien adds that real archaic English is *"far more terse"* than modern (Letter 171), and holds his grand style back for the one turn that needs it (R1).

**The calibration.** Three registers were set on the same three leaves (I.1, IV.3, IV.4) and read by four judges, each through a different lens:
- **A, HEAVY:** Early Modern grammar in every line;
- **B, MEDIUM:** inversions and *ere*, with no old verbs;
- **C, NATIVE:** Orrowen words worked into the English prose.

The votes were **A 3, B 1, C 0.** A alone was really hard and still learnable: its forms were consistent and correct, and its play on *tell* (to count and to recount) teaches itself. B was cosmetic, a first reader goes straight through it, and its Plain twin would have been re-punctuation again. C's native words (*Ston*, *trenn*, *Tumar*, *myst*, *haskard*, *keth*, *lodh*) muddied the scenes and added lore, so they are off. A's own faults became the rules: no creed before the story; no heaviest forms on the hinge words (*clomb*, *tare*, *brake*); no more than about one *-eth* in a hundred words (A had fourteen in 830); no one-off antiquities; and no polish that makes every teller sound alike. The three A leaves, revised to those rules, became the models (`wf11/samples/I1.md`, `IV3.md`, `IV4.md`).

**The rules of the voice** (`voice_v3.md`, binding):
- **HEAVY grammar in every line of narration and speech.** *Thou* for one near hearer, *ye* and *you* for many, for any bonded pair, and for one held in honour. *-eth* for the third person singular present, *hath*, *doth*, *saith*. Strong pasts, one form to a verb Book-wide: the old form for the verbs of the road (*spake*, *sware*, *bare*, *wist*, *bade*, *slew*, *kneeled*, *forgat*) and the shared form for the verbs of the deed (*broke*, *tore*, *climbed*, *struck*, *kicked*, *fell*, *drew*, *took*, *died*). Negation without *do* (*He fought them not.*), except three times a tale for weight. The verb before the subject after a fronted word (*Then laughed his men.*). The object first (*Twelve were we, and the warden.*). *That* for persons. The subjunctive only after *if*, *though*, *lest* and *till*.
- **Short clauses and full stops.** A sentence of the Book may be harder than its Plain twin, but never longer.
- **A few old words, often:** *ere*, *naught*, *yet* (still), *but* (only), *for* (because), *whoso*, *bide*, *feign*, *morrow*, *scarce*, *nigh*, *wherefore*, and the play on *tell* and *told*. Never a one-off antique, and never an invented native word in the prose.
- **Plain at the hinge.** The kick, the tear, the blow, the death and the first instant of a knowing are told in words that 1611 and today share: *He tore it down the back.* · *▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside.* · *Tarnel did not wake.* The archaism lives in the sentences around them.
- **Thin the endings.** One *-eth* to a sentence at most, and rarely more than one in a hundred words. Where a passage wants more, it goes into the past or the plural.
- **One register, many voices** (§1.6).
- **Fixed matter stays outside the dial:** the songs, Jack's lines, the hearth's liturgy, the runners' oath, the Guest's sentence, the warden's letter and the Title. Jack's lore lines are set as quoted formulas after a colon, letter for letter in their own case (*So the terms stand yet, in the guild's own words: with the speed and precision that only Aetherbonded pairs could achieve* · *The slate nameth it so: a rod of polished wood…* · *The Stonwryt the masters call it: a vow sealed in living rock, earned by toil and skill*). His dust line stands as he wrote it: *If you doubt the tale, go and look at the dust on it.*
- **Never:** contractions; *upon* where *on* will do; tushery; scripture formulas; a Christian mark (*God* alone is named); a false friend; a modern word in old dress; American spelling (the warden's *Neutralized* is the one exception, and it is his).

**The measures** (R5's method, tale bodies only; v3 measured the same way for these notes):

| Measure | v1.3 | v2.0 | v3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mean sentence, in words | 15.2 | 21.0 | 10.8 |
| Sentences over 30 words | 9.1% | 24.1% | 0.0% |
| Sentences over 45 words | 1.0% | 12.3% | 0.0% |
| Sentences under 10 words | 39.3% | 34.7% | 49.1% |
| Semicolons a sentence | 0.09 | 0.24 | 0.01 |
| *and* a sentence | 0.69 | 1.04 | 0.39 |
| Old words in 1,000 (with *-eth*) | 0.9 | 9.0 | 11.8 |
| Old grammar in 1,000 (rough) | 0.3 | 1.2 | 3.6 |
| *upon* in 1,000 | 0.57 | 6.34 | 0.04 |
| *the two of them* and *he and she* in 1,000 | 0.09 | 1.26 | 0 |
| Epic similes | 0 | 20 | 0 |
| Contractions | 0 | 0.1 in 1,000 | 0 |
| Headnote, mean words | 82 | 132 | 39 (`count_v3.py`; fifty at most) |

The v3 Book's sentences are a little shorter than v1.3's. The voice guide allows it: old grammar makes a short sentence read slower than its length. Where three short sentences in a row carried no weight, two were joined by an *and* that carries a deed, as v1.3 did.

**What it sounds like.** One passage from IV.3 shows the rules at work: the verb before its subject after a fronted word (*Then took the stranger*), the shared verb at the hinge (*tore*), and the old order that slows the reader again just after it (*Never in his life had he bled.*). A few lines on, the *do*-less negation carries the Guest's stillness: *He lifted not his hand.*

> *Then took the stranger a little blade in his left hand, and touched it to the warden's right arm.*
>
> *▒▒▒▒ tore himself away. Never in his life had he bled. In the tearing, the cut that should have been shallow went deep, and the blood came.*

**The refrains, archaized.** The True Men's refrains and the tellers' marks are not fixed matter, so they follow the grammar. Each is one form Book-wide, and the Plain keeps its own modern form.

| Line | v2.0 | v3 Book | Plain Words |
|---|---|---|---|
| Corlen | *"Wait. The sea has not finished."* | *"Wait. The sea hath not finished."* | *"Wait. The sea hasn't finished."* |
| Hesk | *"Someone has to keep a light."* | *Someone must keep a light.* | *Someone has to keep a light.* |
| Jory | *"It's only a rhyme."* | *"It is only a rhyme."* | *"It's only a rhyme."* |
| Hale's runner-word | *"Hull hangs back."* | *"Hull hangeth back."* | *"Hull hangs back."* |
| the wall's saying | *"For the Captain, an hour lasts as long as a day."* | *"…an hour lasteth as long as a day."* | *"For the Captain, an hour lasts as long as a day."* |
| Kael's mend | *"It is not the world that slows, lad."* | *"It is not the world that sloweth, lad."* | *"It's not the world that slows down, lad."* |
| Orvenna | *"Every gun you haul is a course we do not lay"* | *"Every gun ye haul is a course we lay not,"* / *"so spend a gun as a careful man spendeth silver."* | *"Every gun you haul is a row of stone we don't lay,"* … |
| Halyna to Seren | *"Hold it, and it will hold you."* | *"Hold it, and it will hold thee."* (one near hearer, a frightened child) | *"Hold it, and it will hold you."* |
| Stannard | *"It is not the ice I'm fighting for."* | *"It is not the ice I fight for."* | *"It's not the ice I'm fighting for."* |
| the fleet's fire | *"The fleet has spent its fire."* | *"The fleet hath spent its fire."* | *"the fleet has spent its fire"* |

The rulings behind them: the True Men's refrains are under the dial, not fixed (so *hath* and *must*); the three calls are orders to a battery of many men and are never put into *thou* (*Shift your ground* · *Change your shot* · *Hold, or loose*); *wist* is for a fact and *knew* for a person, a skill or a thing known by touch; *mine* before a vowel or *h* (*mine hand*, *mine own*); *Tidesmeet, where the river meeteth the tide*, since an epithet is a clause and not a name. The Thrones' gloss-names (*the Grain That Rots*) stand as names.

### 1.3 The economy: words per tale

Words per leaf, heading to heading, with datelines, headnotes, songs and the lines after the Amen in, and native blocks and markers out, counted the same way in all three Books. v1.3's single *Book of the Havens* is split into its two parts for III.1 and III.2, and its Homecomings captions are set against VI.1. The last column is the budget measure of the v3 guides: the tale proper, from after the headnote through the hearth's answer, against its ceiling.

| II.1 Of the Keep That Was Forgotten | 769 | 1,568 | 808 | 52% | 770 / 800 |

- **Against v1.3 the Book is 13% longer.** The growth is where v1.3 had no tale to tell: its havens were one catalogue in Halyna's voice and ten one-line captions, and now each True Man swears, falls and comes home in his own voice (III.1, III.2, VI.1). The rest is the beats v2.0 added that Jack keeps: the oils and the Captain's Rite (I.4), the run and the annal (IV.3), the six counts (V.5), two deaths told whole (V.7), the boat (VI.3) and the Epilogue's payoffs.
- **The fixed caps inside a leaf:** a headnote of fifty words or fewer, in one or two sentences; a dateline of eight words or fewer; a wood leaf's reading of eighty words or fewer, counted in its tale; and an annal after another teller's words of 150 or fewer. Every leaf meets every cap.
- **How the words came out** (craft §4.1). The joints, never the beats. Every *", and…; and…"* chain became full stops or lost its weakest clause. Every *As when … so* scaffold became a clause. The tags went (*he and she*, *the two of them*, the epithet where it does not bite). Each beat is told whole once in the Book, and elsewhere it gets a line. The cross-references and explanations went, for the facing leaf does that work in silence. The time-stamps and the *Hear how* openings went. The road is squeezed into a sentence. And every liked element was kept, as a line if it could not be a paragraph.
- **Five leaves are within one per cent of their ceilings:** the foreword (845 / 850), I.5 (843 / 850), III.2 (1,199 / 1,200), IV.3 (1,296 / 1,300) and V.7 (1,289 / 1,300). Anything added to them later must be paid for inside the same leaf.
- **The whole file** is 31,858 words by a plain word count, against v2.0's 53,460.

### 1.4 The stories built as stories

**What the masters do** (craft §0, from the research). Come close at the deed and sum up the road (Tolkien's telescope). Light the deed and leave the heart dark (Genesis 22, read by Auerbach). Build by three, and let the third break (Malory's Bedivere). Plant a thing and pay it without a word (Chekhov; Homer's scar). Hold the high style back for the one moment that needs it. Be colder at the grave. End on a picture. And Jack's last word: this is fiction, and the most fun to write. If a sentence would not have given Seren pleasure to set down, it is not finished.

**The shape of a tale** (craft §2.1):
1. **The call,** in one line: *Give me the stone.* · *Hold the stone, Seren.* · *This is held in the grain.* A teller is a person within three sentences.
2. **The first sentence of story** gives an hour, a place, one sense and the seed of something wrong, and never a date: *At dusk the column came in under the outer gate. I heard it ere I saw it: many feet, and no voice among them.* · *One morning at summer's end, out of the grey came a small grey boat. There was no wind, and the boat came anyway.*
3. **One question by the hundredth word,** a yes-or-no question a child at the front could ask.
4. **The stakes:** one thing to love, shown in action before it is in danger.
5. **The doom,** in a line, if the teller knows it.
6. **The rising,** by three, and the third breaks.
7. **The halt:** one scene, told close and slow, with the shortest sentences of the tale. One object sounds, and nothing is explained.
8. **The turn,** out of what the hearer has already seen. Seren never says what it means.
9. **The cost,** paid in a line.
10. **The closing picture,** then the teller's close and the hearth's answer, and nothing after.

Between any two beats the joint must read *therefore* or *but*. If only *and then* fits, the beat was cut or rebuilt.

**Every tale's spine,** as the v3 Book tells it:

| Tale | The question | The halt | The turn | The close |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I.1 | Will this broken people stand? | the climb with the coal, and *He tore it down the back.* | the small sound of old cloth; then the widening count, till the counter stops: *I stopped. It is the only time I ever stopped.* | *On his back is the other half.* · *He will tell you it was not like that. It was like that.* |
| I.2 | Will a guild sworn never to bear a weapon rise? | the Captain's one question; *No one answered him. I remember the wind in the grass.* | *There was no silence.* | Enno and Della asleep against the new course, their hands joined; *By morning the mortar had set.* |
| I.3 | Will the boy's word reach the Captain in time? | *The Captain standeth still.*, and the hour stretching *like a stone-cart on a long hill* | *"Hold."* … *"Loose."* | Kael's mend: *The moment he chose was the moment that came.* |
| I.4 | Can the Rite be made when the flasks are dry? | the Rite in the thin sun; the hands of Idrenna, that shook at everything else, *On the flasks they shook not.* | *I wist for whom it had been carried.* · *Four hands laid it in two.* | *The people called him Commander. In his own heart he was still only the Captain.* |
| I.5 | Why do we fight for ice and stone? | the Naming in the ward: *"The Commander fought."* / *"The Stonewrights built."* | Ebba's *"Perhaps."*, and *In the morning she did not wake.* | *Tomorrow we go down to the havens.* |
| II.1 | How did the castle that never fell come to ruin? | Halyna turn the fallen capstone at dusk | *It was their own.* · *The stone kept its vow. It was we who did not keep ours.* | *Tonight it lieth under my two hands.* |
| II.2 | What was the grey? | the between-light, and the little ones asleep in the singing | *Ye had never once heard us. We had made sure of it.* | *This is our home…* |
| II.3 | What is the bond, and why is this Book written by one who is not bonded? | the mark drawn once in the ash, and swept out | *When the left hand dies, does not the right one die too?*; then Seren's own: *She woke beside him.* | *Birth united them; death could not separate them.* |
| III.1 | What did we have? | ten men, ten oaths for the homecoming | Stannard swears naught, and knocks once on the hearthstone | *I have no haven.* / *The Captain found me on a road.* |
| III.2 | How did we lose them, and what did each man carry out? | Hesk on the new ice with his mother's lamp: *The ice held.* | the staffs left leaning at the falling walls | *I will not tell you the difference.* |
| IV.1 | What did our fathers do at the edge, and did no one see? | the count of the black wood, ending *He slept in it, and was proud.*; the clerk's line, and the lamp bent to it twice | the letter copied between poles and oars: *What I held, I wist not.* | *The first dark sails were already on the sea.* |
| IV.2 | What did the felling feel like? | the first pillar: *Ere noon it leaned. Yet it held the breath.* / *Then the roots let go.* | the plea cut on the mute thing: *It did not answer.* | the axe in the rings |
| IV.3 | Will anyone at that door do right? | the kneeling, and the three offers refused | *▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside.* · *We obeyed.* · *"Is it so?"* / *"It is written."* | *We were a people of doors.*; the hearth slow to follow; the hood; the runners' four words |
| IV.4 | What did the Guest come to say? | the rite at the door, told as it happened | *And the host drew back. We know not why. Fear leaveth no mark on wood.* · *And we were the one his foot found.* | the sentence, and *That is all he came to say.* |
| IV.5 | Why will the ship-masters not hear it? | Jory's last morning on the east wall, and the tune | *I did not tell him to stop.* | *His is the first cairn on the east face.* · *But Mystwood remembers.* |
| IV.6 | What did the burning boat carry home? | *Then we turned.* | *The iron had gone into us; now we went into the iron.* · *Wood can hide naught.* | *And the roots drank.* |
| V.1 | Will the wood speak? | hands on the plank at the turn of the tide; *"Shore," he said.* | Seren's own palm: *I let go.* | *The word looked very small on its leaf.*, and Halyna's two lines |
| V.2 | What were the young wood's words? | the two ends of the vault, the planks lifted at once | *It said Burn.* · *They had not picked anything.* / *They had been sent.* | *The broken grain met.* · *It is still growing.* |
| V.3 | What does one hull learn, and carry home? | the second coming: *Your wall still flashed.* | *It flashed from a stone along the wall, that had been silent.* | *we came back to look, and died looking* |
| V.4 | Why did they choose war? | the young before the council: *We listened three of their lives…* | *But war is for the short-minded and the angry young.* | the first carving, *Grow until the shore is silent.*; Seren's empty leaf |
| V.5 | What did the war cost, counted? | Count VI: *fourteen names, and twelve to answer* | *It cannot be called home. But it can be sent home.* / *The wood can fear.* | *Tonight it cannot reach his belt.* / *There he sitteth now, at the end of the bench.* |
| V.6 | How is a fleet with no one aboard made, and made to obey? | *The blade went into our heart.* | *No voice among us can call us home.* | the Song of the Years |
| V.7 Part I | Will the two sent apart come back together? | *Then the mallet stopped in the air.* | *She was gone.* · *Ye cannot tell.* · *"One," he said.* | *On Halvard's trowel the mortar was drying.* |
| V.7 Part II | Will the wall see the lie in time? | Pellow on the parapet, and the Captain battery by battery: *One at a time is slow.* | *They learned it from us.* | Ruan's *"Look close."* |
| V.7 Part III | What does the enemy hope for? | the longest knowing | *go home* | *The lamp went out on the shingle, and I filled it not a third time.* |
| VI.1 | Will each man have what he swore? | Hesk on the ice, and Kael counting his steps | *The ice took Hesk too.* … *He came home.* | *Ten havens. I counted them twice, and it took not long.* |
| VI.2 | Who were the ten? | Eirlenth: *no one roofed us* | Neivaere: *ye were hard to look on; and so were we* | *These were the ten…* |
| VI.3 | Is anything in all that wood alive? | *It was green.*; *"Home."* | *We remember our home too.* | the boat turns into the wind, and the ship-masters *did not look away*; the child's *"We remember."* |
| Epilogue | What comes back out of the grey, and who will answer now? | the boat on the shingle; *I knew the bite.* | the Title broken off, with one word too many | *it must be sent kneeling*; the gate left open, and *a child keepeth a light* |
| The Last Note | What do the three grey stones say? | the lamp held to the lowest stone, night after night | three words of ours, by their sound | *I think I have read them. I say I think.* |

**Deaths planted and paid** (craft §2.2). Each death is planted early as a habit, a want or a promise shown in action. It is told cold and short, with no simile and no comment, and a teller who did not see it says so (*I did not see it. Hale did.*). A token outlasts the dead: the glass whole, the lamp burning on the ice, the knot, the frost on both handles. At the cairn or the hearth one line remembers the good. The strip of the cloak is told whole once and then given a clause. **Each death is told whole in one place only**, and elsewhere it gets a line; the ledger is in §3.8.

**The frame, made light** (craft §3):
- **The dateline** names when the tale was laid, in eight words or fewer, in plain italic, never in capitals, and never the date of the events: *The first winter, the night after the Rite.* · *The third winter, in the Tide of Remembering.* The clock of the events is in §3 of these notes, not in the Book.
- **The headnote** tells who laid the tale and adds one human touch. It never holds the ending, a chain of custody, a seal, a rule of the Book, a gloss or the date. v1.3's IV.3 headnote gave the end away; v2.0's ran 175 words on seals and leave. Both are gone.
- **The editorial rules are said once,** in the foreword: the lintel over a pair-name, the two inks, the blot for a cut name, the wood's own names for things, and the bare leaf. *Where the two agree not, we have kept both* is said every night, in the Invocation.
- ***Hear how* openings are gone** (v2.0 had 22). A call to listen that names no outcome stays at the head of a catalogue: *Hear now the names of the havens, and hold them, for they are ours.*
- ***Added in Seren's hand* stays only where the note is itself a turn,** twice in the Book: under Count I, *It was not the last.*; and under VI.1's Rimewatch, the girl who lights the north lamp.
- **Refrains turn, or go.** The liturgy never changes. *And the Captain put his hood back.* is told once as a habit in the foreword (*The Captain will hear all, and tell naught, and keep his hood up.*) and then happens twice: after IV.3, the first Amen over a killing by our own, and in the Epilogue. The refrain that grows instead is the strip of the cloak: told whole at Jory (*It was the first strip.*), then a clause, then *Tonight it cannot reach his belt.*, then *It was the last of his half.* *Hold the stone, Seren.* opens her own leaves bare; its second half, *I tell myself so every time*, is said once (I.4) and turned in the Epilogue (*There is none now to finish it, and I say both halves alone.*). *Look close.* changes hands, from Pellow to Ruan. *I did not understand it then. I think I do now.* is Voss's, once, at Ebba's *Perhaps*.
- **The Arguments** are one line each and spoil nothing: *How we came to stand.* · *Who we were, and who they were.* · *Ten havens, and ten men to tell them: what we had, and how we walked away from it.* · *The wrong, told from both sides of the grey.* · Jack's Book Five line, word for word · *How the havens came home, and the war went home.* · and for the Epilogue, *What the grey sent back.*

**The never-list** (craft §6), in brief: run-on chains and the *As when … so* scaffold; the annal or history-book voice; explanation interrupting a scene; invented custom or lore that explains a deed; summary where a scene belongs; abstraction where a thing would do, and verdict words (*cruel*, *brave*); modern idiom; parody archaism; holy marks; stacked endings after the Amen; and refrains left unchanged.

### 1.5 The wood-leaf form

Jack: *"Halyna speaks in phrase and partial ideas as the wood tells the memory, They are piecing parts together. But Seren, she makes the memory a story."* (K5). In v2.0, Halyna gave the wood in finished paragraphs, a voice to each. In v3 every wood leaf has four parts (voice §5; craft §5; the model is `samples/IV4.md`).

1. **The dateline and the headnote.** Whose hand was on the wood, and where the other of them was, in one or two sentences. Twice a child's question opens it, so the leaf answers someone at the hearth: II.2 (*A child asked whether the Guest's people had houses.*) and IV.4 (*On the night Brenn was read, a child asked what the Guest had given.*). The old closing formula of the wood headnotes (*Told by Halyna, by turns, in two inks…*) is gone; the foreword says it once.
2. **The reading,** under `<!-- READING -->`. Halyna piece the memory together as the wood gives it up: six to nine italic lines, each opening with an em-dash, eighty words at most. The two inks alternate line by line and neither is named. The piecing shows: a wrong image corrected (*Leaves. No — words, like leaves.*), a slip into the wood's *our*, caught (*Our — the wood's kin, in the posts.*), a thought broken across both inks (*Three words in him —* / *— and naught to join them. We two have it.*), and one gap, marked and never mended (*— the blade — [ ]*). It stops at or near the halt, so the story fulfils what the reading could not finish.
3. **The story,** in Seren's hand and the wood's *we*. It opens *This is held in the grain.* and is laid in three rings parted by `<!-- RING -->`. It builds as a stone leaf builds, with one hard image at its centre. A rite is told as it happens and never in advance. It never decodes and never explains a motive.
4. **The return.** *…and the grain holds it still.* Then *And no one answered.*, until VI.3, where a child answers.

**Each reading pieces in its own way,** so they are not one template:

| Leaf | How the reading breaks |
|---|---|
| II.2 | it runs out of words: *Not fog. There is no word —* / *— Breath. We two have it. Breath.*; the name fails at the end: *— [ ]* |
| IV.2 | it counts like a wound (*Strokes. Counted. Every stroke, counted.*), breaks the sky's triple before *wide*, and carries the two drawn marks Halyna could see and could not yet tell |
| IV.4 | the gift's own road: *Hands, warm, a long while. Leaves. No — words, like leaves.*, and the gap at the blade |
| IV.6 | it runs easy and breaks only at the fire: *Fire. Hands burning — no. Boards. The boards burning.* |
| V.3 | it repeats itself, and breaks the second time: *A stone, flashing. Iron, low and —* |
| V.4 | the two inks take the two sides of the council: *Wait —* / *— no. Not wait.* |
| V.6 | the singing stops at the blade: *The singing —* / *— stops. A blade, in the heart.* |
| VI.2 | ten breaths, one sense to a Throne, and the name fails: *Names, under all. Thae — [ ]* |

**What the wood can know** (craft §5.2) is held strictly: touch, weight, cold, fire, blood, iron, the sound that passes through it, a long dark; its kin in sawn timber, and that they are dead; and what came to it through blood or roots. Never a Shore name, a Shore motive or a date. Shore facts go in the headnote, or nowhere, and one facing leaf never explains the other. v3 tightened four places: Seren's Shore lines before each Throne in VI.2 are cut (the facing leaf, VI.1, holds them); *Blackthorn* is out of IV.2's headnote; IV.6's men no longer cast the Guest in *as men cast a net they mean to burn*, which gave them a Shore intent (*as men cast a torn net*); and *Fear leaveth no mark on wood* stands as the wood's refusal at IV.4's hinge.

**The wood's register.** The eldest wood gets the oldest grammar in the Book, object first and the verb last (*Eldest wood are we. A tree we were when the world was green to its edges.* · *Join them he could not.*). Because the wood speaks in the present, it is the voice most at risk of pastiche, so its *-eth* endings are thinned the hardest: *They that come under a roof bring their best* rather than *One that cometh … bringeth*. The ring line stays exact: *and the grain holds it still*, never *holdeth*.

**In the game,** a wood leaf's reading stands where v2.0's IV.2 "fragments" stood. IV.2 shows its reading alone, with its two drawn marks, when the shape of a Blackthorn heart is first known, and its story ripens whole beneath it at Cam 16 (§5.5).

### 1.6 Seren's craft, and the tellers' agreement

Jack: *"Seren loved the language and was able to be playful with it. Turning of phrase. painting pictures with it… She knows how to write them so they sound better. Ever story teller looks are her works and they all agree that her version is the best."* (K5).

**The rule, said once,** in the foreword: *Each night I set the tale in our tongue as masons set a course, every joint broken over the joint below. Each word I say aloud three times ere I write it, to hear whether it will hold. The tale is the teller's. The words are mine. I read each leaf to him that laid it, and no teller yet hath asked for his own back.* And four words of her own: *I love our tongue.*

**The agreement, shown and never stated:**
- **I.1, the headnote:** *He forbade me to change one word of it; I changed a good many, and when I read it him, he sware it was word for word as he had told it.*
- **I.3, the headnote:** *He told it standing, and as fast as ever he ran; I have set it down slower.*
- **V.5, the headnote, six winters on:** *In the sixth winter he bade me mend all six, as I had mended his first; I have mended them less.*
- **II.3:** Idrenna, by the door, *nodded not*; and at the end, when Seren's pen has stopped and started again, *By the door, Idrenna nodded.*
- **VI.1, the headnote:** Kael *counted my leaves ere he would begin.*

**Her economy** is §1.3. **Her play** is words that work twice, set up once and then let bite:

| Word | Its two senses | Where it bites |
|---|---|---|
| *lay*, *laid* | stone laid in a wall; a tale laid at the hearth | the foreword (*A tale that is laid is part of the wall.*); every close |
| *tell*, *told* | to count; to recount | Kael: *A teller am I, and no singer. Heads I tell, and stones, and days. What I told not, that will I not tell.* |
| *course* | a row of stone; a night's tale | the founders' leaf (*one course each night*); the house of one door, *One course high it stood* |
| *hold* | to keep in the hand; the third hour of the siege day; to keep in memory | *Hold it, and it will hold thee.* · *hold them, for they are ours* · *and the grain holds it still* |
| *keep* | a vow, a light, a count; the Keep | *The stone kept its vow. It was we who did not keep ours.* · *a child keepeth a light* · *I keep this Book* |
| *mark* | a pair's Stonwryt; marks on the wood; the mark on an arm | *Marks on the wood.* · *The warden's arm bare one mark* |
| *sentence* | words joined; a doom | IV.4: *We were the sentence.* · V.1: *three words, and no sentence* |
| *spend* | a fleet's fire; silver; a gun | *spend a gun as a careful man spendeth silver* · *The fleet hath spent its fire.* |
| *sent*, *called* | sent home; called home | *It cannot be called home. But it can be sent home.* · *it must be sent kneeling* |
| *door* | a house's door; the law of the guest | *We were a people of doors.* · the house of one door · the gate left open |
| *seed* | the winged seed of war; the green sliver | VI.3: *So it was seed.* |
| *ink* | Two-Inks; one ink | the Epilogue: *I write this alone, in one ink* · *Dry on my table stand their two inks.* |
| *leaf*, *leaves* | a tree's leaf; a page | IV.4: *leaf on leaf* · VI.3: *not a leaf had the pillars put up* |
| *name* | the name cut in; the name cut out | *The guild buried him under his name. The warden's name it cut away.* |
| *but*, *on* | only and however; onward and on him | *words without sentences are but sounds, and we heard but sounds* · *▒▒▒▒ screamed on, and some went on* |

**Her turns,** each tale's own: the mirror (*I carried the lie in a day and a night. I carried the truth forty years, and gave it to no one.*); the litany that ends on the wrong thing (*He slept in it, and was proud.* · *And no one read them.*); the look by three that ends on the one no one looked at (*To the Captain looked none of them.*); the withheld thought (*I have written the name, and I will not write what I thought.*); the epithet turned (Kael the Counter stops counting; Voss who asked answers; Seren Two-Inks writes at last in one ink); the weak man in one clause (*▒▒▒▒ stood within, and came no further.*); the refusal at the hinge (*We know not why. Fear leaveth no mark on wood.*); and the saying at the close (*We were a people of doors.*).

**Her painted pictures,** one to a movement, fifteen words or fewer, from a trade the teller knows: *It is a sound like ice going out on a river.* · *like mallets in a far quarry* · *as gleaners along a stubble-field* · *the hall fell quiet, as a street doth for a bier* · *like a stone-cart on a long hill* · *as a fisher casteth a torn net into the bilge* · *A fledgling could have slept in that hand.* · *as the halves of a split log meet* · *like teeth from a jaw* · *as wives look up when men long in fever ask for their boots*. She plays on the road and goes plain at the grave: none of these stands at a kick, a blow, a death or the first instant of a knowing.

**Her habit,** saying a word three times before she trusts it to the ink, is shown whole once (V.1, *shore*) and echoed twice (VI.3, *Naelear*; the Knowings).

**Every teller lifted, and still himself** (voice §4). Seren's polish never flattens a man:

| Teller | His sentence | His marks | What Seren gives him | Never |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Kael** (I.1, III.1, III.2, V.5, VI.1) | the curtest in the Book; numbers first; he counts, names, swears and corrects | *I swear to that.* · *I counted us twice, and it took not long.* · *It was like that.* · *A count is no count till it be named.* | one picture a tale he would not have made (*ice going out on a river*), and the play on *tell* | a simile of his own; anything lyric; a gloss |
| **Hale** (I.3) | short of breath; he slips into the present at the run, for three to five sentences | *I am not a teller. I run.* · *I ran it.* · his three-word messages | the slowed hour, told in his own things (a cart on a hill; ripples counted like a log's rings) | reflection longer than a line; anything he did not see |
| **Voss** (I.5, IV.5) | he asks, circles back in the same words, and says when he tells at second hand | *Ye may weigh it.* · *So my grandmother told it me.* · *I did not understand it then. I think I do now.* (once) | the ring (IV.5 opens and closes on a screaming hull), and the rhyme turning in him | certainty; a custom of the coast; a moral at the close |
| **Brenn** (IV.3, read by Halyna) | short, guilty sentences; he weighs small facts and returns to what he cannot put down | *The blows I counted. I count them yet.* · *Why I did it, I know not.* · *I know it now.* | the mirrored confession | a blow of his own; a laugh of his own; a reason for another man's deed |
| **Corlen** (III.1, III.2, through V.5) | the fewest words; the sea is the subject of his sentences | *"Wait. The sea hath not finished."*; the wet finger | the evening tide coming in over the ash | hurry; the first shot |
| **The True Men** (III.1, III.2, VI.1) | one man, one image, one want, in one to three sentences | Tamm *One span is a plank. Three is a city.* · Pellow *"Look close."* · Hesk *Someone must keep a light.* · Harl *"Burn it."*, then *I did not burn it.* | each man's oath, set so VI.1 can pay it | a tag at a climax; a boast over the fallen |
| **Halyna**, on a stone leaf | balanced halves in two inks, one beginning and the other ending, with plural verbs and *we two* | the split line (*"A tale told from one side of a wall,"* / *"is half a tale."*) | the second half that turns the first | a tag on every line; *he and she*; a singular verb |
| **Halyna**, reading the wood | fragments, corrections, gaps, mostly nouns, in the present | *We two have it.* | nothing: Seren sets their pieces down as they fell | a sentence of explanation; anything the Shore knows |
| **The wood**, as Seren tells it | the eldest grammar; *we*; *ye* to the Shore; things by its own names (the breath, the iron, the mute thing, the long-lived, shore-men, the host) | *This is held in the grain.* … *and the grain holds it still.* | the ring, with one hard image at its centre | a Shore name, word or motive |
| **Seren** | a little longer, twelve to sixteen words, with one painted picture to a movement | *Hold the stone, Seren.* | her own | a lecture on a word; a footnote to her own Book; a verdict; a decoding of the facing leaf |

The lesser voices keep one mark each: Idrenna speak slowly, in halves, and nod or do not nod; Orvenna never raise their voices; Ebba says *"Perhaps."*; Merrick says *"Not that one."*; the Captain asks one question in I.2 and gives the three calls, and says nothing else; ▒▒▒▒ has two screams, one order and one letter, and no speech at all.

### 1.7 IV.3: Brenn watched, and the warden burned the boat

**Brenn only watched** (K7). Every deed of the killing is *they*. His hands touch only the runners' stone and, the next morning, the gift.

| v2.0 | v3 |
|---|---|
| *I struck him with the pole I pushed the boats off with. Twice. I have counted them every night since.* | *They took up what lay to hand: staves, and stones of the shingle, and the boat-poles. One of them took up my pole, the pole I pushed the boats off with.* · *The blows I counted. I count them yet.* |
| *We seized the stranger…* · *We had him by both his arms* | *"Seize him," he screamed.* / *They seized him. He fought them not.* |
| *And his men laughed. I laughed. I have not laughed at anything since.* | *Then laughed his men. I stood among them, and I hear that laughter yet.* · and of the axemen that autumn: *I laughed not, for I had seen it turn. Yet I said naught. The Council had it in writing.* |
| *We set the boat alight… We shoved it off* | *They set it alight… With the same poles they shoved it off.* |
| — | *His men obeyed.* / *We obeyed.* / *I say* we, *for the post was one thing that day, and I was of it. No hand laid I on him. I did not stop them. A boy was I among the men.* |

His sins are enough without a blow: he stood among the laughter; he did not stop them; he carried the gift and the lie in one pack; he answered *"It is written."*; and he kept the truth forty years. *Forty years have I weighed that small cleverness.* Whether he laughs with the men is still Jack's to say (§7.2 call 1).

**The warden is a weak man, shown only by his deeds** (K8), and never judged:
- *Black wood had ▒▒▒▒ set everywhere: in the doorposts, in his table, in the frame of his bed.*
- *He held the post by his father's name on the Trade Council, and was scarce older than his runner. For his temper they had sent him to the edge, where there were but the twelve of us for it to wound. He had never known battle. He had never known patience.*
- *▒▒▒▒ stood within, and came no further.* · *▒▒▒▒ kicked it aside.* · *▒▒▒▒ stood and let it be done.*
- *▒▒▒▒ tore himself away. Never in his life had he bled.*
- the two screams, *"Seize him."* and *"Kill him."*; then *▒▒▒▒ held his arm, and looked not at the boat.*
- the order: *"Burn it," said he, "and shove it off, and give it the tide. Leave naught to show."*
- the letter; the long sleeves *the rest of his life, through the hot summers of Eldhythe*; *Last winter he died in his bed of black wood.*

**The burning is his order, to hide the deed.** The scream in the fire is the wood's, and Brenn reaches it by weighing facts: *There was a screaming in it. The stranger was dead. It was no man that screamed. It was the wood itself.* The irony the Book never says is stronger than the custom ever was: the warden burned the boat to hide what was done, the burning boat carried the whole deed home in its boards, and the cover-up is what told the council. IV.6 answers *Leave naught to show* with *Wood can hide naught.*

**Tobe** stays for what he does, and says nothing: *Old Tobe had fished the edge thirty years.* · *Old Tobe went into the sea to his knees and washed the poles.*

**Other v3 beats of IV.3.** The Guest *set it down as ye would set down bread before a guest*; a gull walks among the stones and is gone when it is quiet; the blade is *yet in his hand, turned to his own arm*, which Brenn sees and does not understand (the wood alone names the cut that was never made, in IV.4); the boat comes with *no wind* and turns *Into the wind … and against the current, and into the grey, burning*. The letter is word for word. Seren's annal is 149 words: the half-mark on the warden's arm (*the half of something, cut off in the middle*, never a holy sign); *The Council kept the letter.*; *Brenn died, as a man setteth down a load.*; the name cut; Wendhessa's silence; the chest's two handles; *On the night of the pass Wendhessa lay down, and rose not.*; *At morning frost lay on both.*; *Tarnel did not wake.* Then Halyna's *"She carried it every step,"* / *"and after the passes she carried it alone."*, the law of the door, and the one closing gesture: the hearth slow to follow, the hood, and the runners' oath sworn again with four words, *Not as Brenn carried.*, which *Wick, the youngest, sware … loudest*.

**Retired outline lines** (`wf9/outline.md`, as R6b §0.1 lists them): §0 l.93; §1 l.130; §5.2 l.516 (Brenn's roll, *struck the Guest twice*) and l.517 (Tobe's custom); §7.9 whole; IV.3 scene 11 and its seeds; V.1's seeds; VI.3 l.2186 and l.2205; the Epilogue's l.2268. J18 (*the burning of the boat has a reason*) is still honoured. The reason is the warden's.

### 1.8 The custom removed, and how each later tale was mended

v2.0 gave the coast a custom of burning what the grey sends, spoken by old Tobe in IV.3 and echoed in four later tales. Jack: *"There is no customs of burn the boat… This is the kind of stuff that muddies the story."* (K8). A search of the v3 Book finds the word *custom* nowhere. Every trace, and its mend:

| Where | What v2.0 had | What v3 does |
|---|---|---|
| **IV.3** | Tobe's saying of the coast; the men glad of it, for it let them stop thinking; the warden glad, for the body went out with the boat | the burning is ▒▒▒▒'s order, given in a weak man's panic, holding his arm: *"Burn it," said he, "and shove it off, and give it the tide. Leave naught to show."* Tobe washes the poles and says nothing |
| **IV.5** | the ship-masters ended on *the old words of the coast*, kept for that boat; ill luck within sound of the sea | the masters' fear rests only on what the survivors spread: Daveth home on a spar, the outpost men's oaths in their cups, two boats grown to twenty, and *the boat that cometh for you when ye die*. *Which no boat can do* is a sailor's wonder at the turning, not a belief. The cities' writing and the rhyme describe the warden's deed and nothing more |
| **V.1** | the soldiers burned the first wreck *by the old way of the coast*, saying the old words over it | three deeds, and nothing said over the fire: plain fear (*The old sailors on the wall would not go nigh it.*), want of wood (*The soldiers went, and made their fires of it along the tide-line, for wood was dear on the ridge.*), and one man's hatred (*"Burn it," said Harl of the burning shore. He broke a rib of it across his knee, and fed it in.*). *The Captain let them burn the most of it, and stood among them, still.* |
| **V.2** | every plank in the vault had been saved from the custom | *The soldiers' fires had the rest, and the tide.* |
| **VI.3** | *nor did anyone bring fire to the shingle* | cut: with no custom, no one would bring fire to a sending, and the line could only be heard as a trace of the old rite. The turn is IV.3's sentence without its last word: *Into the wind it went, and against the current, and into the grey.* The old ship-masters, who *Six winters had … turned their faces from the sea when a hull burned*, *did not look away* |
| **the Epilogue** | a ship-master said the custom over a beached grey boat; a soldier ran for fire; the children reached it first; the brand was quenched in the sea | the fire beat is dropped (R6b's option ii; R6b's keep of *the brand quenched in the sea* yields to Jack's fix). *The old ship-masters stood at the head of the shingle, and came no further.*: the warden's own posture at his door, set down without a word, and the children walk past it. Merrick's hand on the gunwale breaks it. The held moment is given at the halt instead: *The rain had stopped. Grey as slate lay the sea, and the tide drew back with a long sound.* |
| **IV.6** | *as men cast a net they mean to burn* | *as men cast a torn net*, which rhymes with IV.3's *as a fisher casteth a torn net into the bilge* and gives the men no intent before the order |
| **III.1** | no trace in v2.0, but a first v3 draft gave Harl *Aske may lay his hand on the planks. I say burn them.* | cut as invented lore. His oath ends on his own temper: *the grey I will burn, and the sea too, if it would take fire.* Aske's wreck-wood burns at the hearth as the night's fuel, and nothing says why |
| **I.3** | Jory whistles *a tune that the old ship-masters hate* | kept: the hate is plain fear, which IV.5 grounds in Daveth |
| **IV.1** | *After the year of the burning boat no crew would row within sight of the grey* | kept, as *and the rolls say not why*: it was plain fear already, and it is the root of every mend |
| ***The Grey Boat*** | *Burn it and shove it and give it the tide* | verbatim, as every song is. It now keeps the warden's own words without knowing it |

**Harl's *Burn it* is one man's temper, never a custom**, and it turns once, by a deed:
1. III.1: *the grey I will burn, and the sea too, if it would take fire*;
2. III.2: of the Emberhythe mason's charred plank, *I told him to burn it.*;
3. V.1: *"Burn it,"* of the first wreck, and the rib across his knee;
4. VI.1: at Emberhythe, of that same plank on the black crust, *I did not burn it.*;
5. VI.3: *Burn it, he had said of every plank of the war. This one he set down at Tarnard's feet, and said naught.* Then he walks the bow into the tide.

**Fire that stays, and why each is safe:** the warden's order; the rhyme; the fire-ships of the siege, which scream as they come; the soldiers' fires of wreck-wood (fear, want of wood and Harl's hatred); the hulls that burn and the ship-masters who turn their faces; and the fear of grey boats, which three men from a spar and the outpost's men in their cups spread along the coast.

### 1.9 The memory theme

Jack, 2026-10-02: *"Yes. Include them. It seems like we already have the elements and this just adds nuance to the story. That is the exact thing I was looking for."* Four nuances, woven into what the Book already held. No new custom, no lore, no modern voice and no lecture: the tellers and Seren feel them and never argue them.

| The nuance | Where the Book holds it |
|---|---|
| **1. Remembering is chosen, not suffered.** *We remember* is a vow the hearth makes. | the foreword, once, in Seren's words: *Forgetting came on our fathers, and none chose it. Remembering we choose, as masons choose which stones to keep.* · III.1: the hearth's answer comes after ten sworn oaths, so it is heard as a vow, and nothing says so · the Invocation: *We lay these tales, for once we forgat.* |
| **2. A people is what it remembers, and forgetting brings ruin.** | IV.1: *Then the havens forgat. … children asked what they said, and were told naught. Some of you that sit at this fire were those children.* · IV.3: Brenn's *I carried the truth forty years, and gave it to no one.*, and the annal's cold *The Council kept the letter.*, where the reader supplies *and forgat the Guest* · IV.5: Jack's *We forget. We have always been good at that. But Mystwood remembers.* · V.4: *The elders' words went not into the moss with them.* · the Last Note: *so was I answered when I was small*, and the litany that ends *And no one read them.* |
| **3. Memory handed down as rite.** | the Invocation: *Many hands have held it, and many shall. Whoso holdeth it now is the teller.* · every close: *This I lay as it was laid for me.* · I.3: *Every haven had its runners' stone. Ours we made the first week… and on it we sware the old oath* · I.2: the old gate-song at the first course of the war · *Hear it now, and hold it, for it is ours.* (IV.1) and *hold them, for they are ours* (III.1, V.5, VI.1), answered by the wood's *Hold them, for they were ours.* (VI.2) · VI.3: a child answers the wood first · the Epilogue: *I read it out to the children.* · *Grief is an inheritance, and it is paid out to the children.* · *on the north wall a child keepeth a light* |
| **4. Remember the good, not only the loss.** | V.5 Count VI: *Jory, that whistled. Corlen, that waited on the sea. Wick, that ran everywhere. Enno and Della, Enrella, hand in hand. Pellow, that held his glass up to everything.* · IV.5: *Slow he whistled it, as he had turned the rope for the least of us.* · IV.3's annal: *his hand on one handle, hers on the other* · *Tarnel knotted his net with his free hand. I watched the knots.* · III.2: *Their cloaks they had given the children.* · I.5: *Most nights she began it, a breath before the rest.* · the Epilogue: *The night I laid my first tale, I was afraid to speak, and Halyna laid my two hands on the stone.* · VI.1: every man's oath paid in his own sense, the dead men's by the men who speak for them |

### 1.10 Plain Words, a true retelling

Jack: *"The Plain Word is just a change in punctuations with a little rephrasing. I wan t there to be a real difference… The Plain Word should be easy to read."* (K4). R5 measured why: both earlier Plains followed their Books paragraph for paragraph (408 to 407, and 983 to 983), and v2.0's Plain shared 40.3% of its four-word runs with its Book. The old Modern v1.2, which felt so different, shared 65.8%: it felt different only because its own register was modern, and word for word it was closer to its Book than v2.0's Plain was to its own.

**How it was made** (voice §11, binding; the model is `samples/I1_plain.md`). For each leaf: list every beat, fact, name, number, line of speech and carrying picture; close the Book; tell the tale from the list as this teller would tell it today to a hall of tired people, in his own sentences and paragraphs; check that nothing is lost and nothing added; check the fixed matter word for word. The rules: common words, glossed once in passing where needed (*the Stonewrights, our builders*); normal word order, with no inversion; no archaism at all; contractions where a speaker would use them; short sentences that flow, joined with *and*, *but*, *so*, *because* and *when*; no semicolons; a figure's meaning kept and its shape let go; no picture added; nothing explained that the Book leaves dark; and every lock of the Book held.

**What it keeps exactly:** the Title and its capitals; Jack's lines; *Stop… Sky… Dying…*; the warden's letter; the runners' oath and *Not as Brenn carried.*; *This is held in the grain.* and *And no one answered.*; the liturgy in its Plain form (*I have laid this tale the way it was laid for me.* · *And everyone at the hearth said: We remember.* · *and the grain still holds it*); the Plain verse of each song as v2.0's Plain had it; the pair-names under their lintel with plural verbs; every blot; every marker; and every native block. It has no datelines, and its headnotes are one or two plain sentences of 45 words or fewer.

**The difference, shown** (I.1):

| The Book | Plain Words |
|---|---|
| *The people looked for a general, and the generals were slain. They looked for the lords, and the lords were scattered. They looked to the Stonewrights, and the Stonewrights sat in the grass with their chisels in their laps.* / *To the Captain looked none of them.* | *People wanted a leader, but the generals were dead, the lords had scattered, and even the Stonewrights, our builders, sat in the grass holding their chisels.* / *Nobody looked at the Captain.* |

**The check numbers.** `plain_check.py`, run on every leaf of the assembled `plain_v3.md` against its leaf in `book_v3.md`. Length is the tale proper; sentence measures are for prose, without fixed matter or readings; overlap is the share of the Plain's four-word runs found anywhere in its Book leaf, all text and prose alone.

| Leaf | Book | Plain | Plain / Book | Paragraphs, Book → Plain | Mean sentence | Under 10 words | Contractions in 1,000 | Grade (FK) | Overlap, all / prose |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Of This Book | 845 | 716 | 84.7% | 27 → 21 | 12.6 | 35% | 22.3 | 3.8 | 13.0% / 13.0% |
| I.1 | 1,076 | 912 | 84.8% | 36 → 28 | 12.1 | 37% | 27.4 | 3.1 | 16.4% / 14.3% |
